#The Digital Logos Workstation

#Part One — The instrument

#1. Hearing it together

Human knowledge has never been more abundant, or more estranged from itself. The fields that know the most barely speak to one another. A physicist and a contemplative can spend their lives on the same question — what it is to be aware of anything at all — and never read a line of each other's work. The traditions that carried wisdom for thousands of years are shelved under religion, the sciences sit in their departments, and a person's own experience is filed under nothing at all. AI could help. Mostly it is being used to make more text, faster than anyone can read it.

Lay the fields side by side, though, and they are often saying the same thing in different keys. Kepler heard the planets as harmony and computed their orbits in the same breath. When this project worked through the structure of meaning carefully enough, it found the basic intervals of music already inside it. Knowledge has a musical shape: separate voices that can be heard together without being merged into one. What has been missing is an instrument for hearing them that way, and for playing them.

Music got its instrument. Making a record once took a studio, an engineer and someone willing to pay for the hours; Ableton Live put recording, arranging, mixing and performing into one window with one clock, and now someone with a laptop can make a record in their bedroom and have strangers dancing to it by the weekend. Live did not make music easy. It made the whole craft reachable, with controls you learn once and then play with everything you have.

The Digital Logos Workstation is that instrument for knowledge, and it is built on Live's own grammar. You can read a source and keep its thread to the last citation. You can set agents to build something and watch them work. You can give an idea a form you can see and hear, and build what you find into a world someone else can walk into. And because the person doing all this is also a world — with depths that never quite rise into words — the instrument holds both: the whole that can be understood, and the self who understands it.

It isn't easy. Working this way takes reading, patience and a willingness to be corrected by your sources. But when the voices start to sound together, there is nothing like it.

#2. Worlds

Everything in the DLW is a world, and every world is drawn as a body in space.

When you open it you see your own from close orbit: a planet turning slowly under its star, with the things you are living with gathered around it — the agents at work for you, the moments still open from today, the scenes you made last week, the people you have been talking to. Come down to the surface and the places on it are the things you are making. A project is a place on the ground, with paths worn between its landmarks; a book you are writing is another; a practice you keep to yourself is a room fogged to everyone but you. The day's work leaves tracks across the surface. Lighter things stay up in orbit, the files and context you have to hand right now, and drift off when you put them down.

Choose its seas and continents, how rugged the coasts run, how warm and wet the climate is, what colour the sky turns at evening, and reroll the seed if you want a new body from the same settings. Then place things on it and name them: a place, a palace you can walk into, a road between two of them, a portal through to someone else's world. Most of the building happens without your noticing. Every project you start becomes a place, and every walk you take wears a path.

The same holds in every kind of work. In Factory a task is a small world being made, its agents circling it and the helpers they spin up riding smaller orbits inside theirs, so that a whole programme of work hangs in front of you as a system you can take in at a glance. A song you are making in Live is a world, its parts the bodies in it. You can go from your day to the work you commissioned to the song you are making and never learn a new way to look.

Earth is there too, the one world nobody built. It carries real geography, and anything in your own world can be anchored to it: a letter to the town it was posted from, an idea to the house where someone first had it, a walk to the coastline it followed. You cannot reshape Earth. You place things on it, as people always have.

A collaborator's planet turns nearby. A research group is a system of planets around a shared star, and a whole discipline is a field you can pull back far enough to see entire, with the relations between worlds drawn across the dark — one anchored to another, one taking part in another, one watching, a contribution crossing the gap like a particle, two fields joined at a remove. How far you look widens a step at a time: your world alone, your world with the projects and agents it holds, the shared field, the fields that field is joined to. Whatever lies just past the edge of your view is counted and offered.

What do others see? Only what you show them. You can look at your own world as yourself, as the field sees it, or as the public sees it, and whatever you have not shown lies under cloud; raising anything into wider view asks you first. Visit someone else's world and you land on what they have shown, tinted and clouded as they left it, with the agent that keeps it there to talk to. Being findable is not being reachable. Being reachable is not being in touch, and being in touch is not being trusted — each step is separate, and each is the owner's to give. If the other world's agent gives you something, it arrives in your world under their name, and stays theirs until you have made it your own.

So one person's work and everyone's knowledge turn out to be the same kind of thing. A private journal, a team's codebase, an archive of letters, a school of thought, the literature of a whole science: each is a world at its own distance, and you move between them by looking further out or closer in. The personal and the academic are near and far in one sky. You build your world by working in it, and as it grows it becomes something others can see, walk through and learn from.

Over every world there is a sky, and the sky belongs to the world. Its planets move as the real ones do; stand in today's, or in the sky of any day you choose. Two people under the same sky receive the same movements and answer them differently, because each of them is a sky as well, a whole system turning inside a person.

We are all, in a sense, aliens to one another.

#3. One grammar

If you have used Live, you already know how to hold the DLW. If you haven't, the grammar is small enough to learn in an afternoon, and once learned it does not change from one kind of work to the next. Only what it holds changes.

Start with a track. In Live a track is a lane for one kind of sound — drums, a bassline, a voice — and it plays one clip at a time. In the DLW a track is a lane for one stream of your work. In Central the day is the set and each world you work in is a track — your own, a project's, a book's — and every world sends its work to the Day, the way every Live track sends its sound to the Main track. In Factory each agent session is a track. In Expressions each Expression is one. In Technē a track might be the thread of a single reading of history, or every place a subject turned up, in order.

On the track sit clips, the pieces you actually handle. A Live clip is a melody, a drum pattern, a whole song. A DLW clip is an entry in your day, a task an agent is working on, a scene in an Expression, an event at its year and place. Every clip shows its state — waiting, running, stopped, recording, stalled — so you can read a whole grid at a glance and know where things stand.

Live calls a row across the tracks a scene, and launching it starts one clip on every track at once. The DLW keeps the row and calls it a stop, because here scene always means an Expression's scene. A stop stands several things together. In Factory, firing a row launches a batch of tasks across sessions. In Technē, a stop on a walk stands a place, a moment, and the scenes that belong there. In Central, the day's face stands the entries you made while it was turned up. A stop is how you set several things going as one move.

Clips and stops live in two views of the same material. Session view is the grid: tracks across, rows down, everything launchable, nothing in any fixed order. It is where you try things, fire them, put this beside that and see what happens — a batch of tasks, a run of scenes, three passages and an answer. Arrangement view lays the same material along one ruler, so you can see the course of it: today from first entry to last, every thread of agent work with a needle showing where it has got to, an Expression's scenes in order with their sound beneath them, history from deep time to this morning. Tab moves you between the two.

Select a clip, and the bottom of the window shows it in detail. Live splits that area in two, and so does the DLW. Clip view shows the thing itself, with everything you can do to it. Device view shows what is working on it.

Clip view is where the hands get busy. In Live it holds a clip's name and colour, when and how it launches, what follows it, where it starts and ends and loops, and an editor for its notes or its sound. Each of those has a counterpart. Open a scene in Expressions and you get a roll where symbols run in lanes like notes, each step's hold and change set like a note's length and glide, with the scene's sound shown beneath it. Open a task in Factory and you get its log, every tool the agent used laid out along the task's time like notes on a bar, with what happens after it finishes and when your Stop will land. Open an event in Technē and you see its span, what it gathered, and how sure anyone is of it. Open an entry in Central and you are simply in the document, writing.

Device view shows the chain: the things working on the material, left to right, in the order the material passes through them. In Central that means the packet, the agent that answers you, and what it may touch. In Factory it is the agent's whole path — the way in, the agent, its skills, the part of the world it may touch, and the place its work lands. In Expressions it is the studio that shapes the field. Wherever you are, the instrument in the focused pane brings its own controls into the chain.

A few of Live's quieter habits come through intact, and you will lean on them. A follow action decides what comes after a clip — the next task, the next stop, the next scene — so that things run on without you pressing each one. Launch quantize decides when a launch lands: right away, at the next bar, or, in Factory, once the agent finishes the tool it is using. Takes stack under a clip so you can choose the one that stands: the best capture of a scene, the attempt at a task that should land, the reading of a history you want to keep. Above all of it runs the transport, the same controls in the same places in every mode, each meaning what it should for the work in front of you.

#4. Three times

Whatever you are working with, you can meet it in three times.

Live is the thing as it happens: agents moving around the task they are building, the field breathing on its pulse, your planet changing under your hands. It is Session view's time — things launched and running, you listening and answering.

Take it into your hands and it is composed. You order the stops of a walk, build a scene until the figure says what you meant, drag a graph into the shape of an argument. That is Clip view's work, and you can do it in the present or step back into a past moment, a chosen day's sky, and compose there.

What you keep is recorded — a take, a walk laid along the ruler, a day closed into what it taught. It sits in Arrangement view to be edited, played back, and given to someone who will play it in their own world, where it is live again.

#5. Central — the day's work

Central is where the day happens. It looks and works like a code editor — your files down the left, documents open side by side, a terminal a click away — except that the day itself is one of the documents, and it fills itself in. Everything you do across the DLW leaves its mark there as you go: the passages you read and marked, the tasks you set going in Factory and what came back from them, the scene you composed, the walk you recorded, the conversation you had in someone else's world. By evening the day has gathered the whole of it into one structured page, and you add what only you can — what you noticed, what didn't work and why, what you want to remember. The work and your account of the work end up in the same place, and the evening never has to reconstruct the morning.

Today is a set, as a song is in Live, and every world you touch today has a track in it. Say you are writing a book. Its world is a track, and the people in it, the objects and sources it turns on and the conversations you have about it are that track's clips. Open the chapter's flow and it plays, with the agent you were talking to back in its seat; open a person and you can read them, walk to where they lived, or find them in the sky. Whatever is still playing is what is open now. It carries into tomorrow's set until you close it, while everything you opened and wrote lays itself down along the day's timeline, so that by evening the Day's arrangement is the day as you lived it. The Day itself sits at the end of the grid, where Live keeps its Main track, and every world sends its work there.

How you work is kept apart from what you work on. Under the browser, where Live keeps its grooves, the pool holds your workspaces: each remembers which panes are open, which instruments, which widgets, and the layout every mode opens with. Launch one and all of it comes back at once, in Central and in every other mode.

Every document in Central starts from a template, and every template is yours to change. A Day is shaped like a die, four faces for the work and two at its edges, and you turn it face by face as the day moves. A Flow is a running conversation or journal, with the people and agents in it named at the top. Beings and Things are the people and objects your work is about, each tied to the world it belongs to, its sources, and the real place where it stands. Restyle them, add sections, build new kinds of your own. However a page looks, it keeps its connections: a Day you have redesigned still fills itself, and a Thing you have rearranged still knows where it stands on the map.

Type @ and you are speaking Central's own search language. At its simplest it finds anything you can reach — a person, an agent, a place, a document, a passage — and sends what you have selected there. Highlight a paragraph and tag a colleague, and it reaches them as a message carrying the passage and where it came from. Tag an agent and it starts working on it. Tag a place and the passage is filed on that spot of your world. Past the simple uses it is a language with a grammar you grow into, and you make your own aliases in it, so that a short name you chose stands for a long search you use every day. Like anything worth learning on an instrument, it rewards the hours you give it.

Select a passage and the rest is close at hand: highlight it, note it, put it in the packet, or ask about it now. The packet is exactly what the agent will read; you see it whole, and how big it is, before anything is sent. Press Play and the agent answers into the page as a new entry with its sources, and any source followed back lands you on the line it came from. Press Record and talk, and your words go into the document. Overdub decides whether what comes back joins as a new entry or replaces the one you selected.

The agent never writes behind your back. With Arm off, anything it wants to change arrives as a proposal; arm it, and its edits land directly, each with a receipt. Change a passage the agent wrote and a light comes on, so that one press can hand it back. Tap stamps the moment into whatever you have open.

Turn to the world instrument and Central is your planet. Projects are places on its surface. The day's work is a walk across it, marked wherever something mattered, and whatever you are holding — open files, the context gathered for an agent — circles overhead and drops away when you are finished with it. On the world's graph side you see what today touched; in the knowledge graph you can follow your notes link by link into everything you have already written.

Then the day closes itself. What you learned is filed into your knowledge, what you made is kept, and tomorrow begins on a clean face of the die.

#6. Factory — worlds making worlds

Factory is where you work with AI agents. Say what you want built — software, a body of research, a place for others to stand in — and the work breaks into tasks that agents take up while you watch the floor, stepping in where you are needed and staying out of the way where you aren't.

Each agent works a track, and its tasks are the clips. Drag an agent from the browser onto an empty track and it takes the seat. Fire a task and it runs; fire a row and a batch starts across every session at once. How a task is drawn tells you how it stands. A running task has no end, because nobody knows yet how long it will take, and a queued one has no length at all. A stalled task is striped and frozen where its output stopped, and its track lights a retry. Groups of agents fold under one header, and each agent's helpers step in beneath it, a coloured rail for every level, so three layers of delegation read at a glance.

In Arrangement view the work moves. Every thread runs along one ruler with a needle that jumps each time its agent checks in, and a needle that stops moving is a stall. Under each thread run its messages, the files it touched, its terminal, the pages it read; click any of them and it opens beside you.

Select a session and its chain shows the road the work travels, left to right. In through a gateway, which decides what the agent must ask your permission for. Then the agent itself: its model, how much it decides alone, how hard it thinks, how many turns it gets. Then its skills, racked with a light each: you ask for a skill on or off, the rack shows whether it is loaded and what it has done, and a preview shows the change before you apply it. Then the part of the world it may touch, from one object up to everything you own, and whether what it writes there arrives as a proposal, a draft or a change. Last, the place its work lands: commits, and a request to merge with the checks that tested it. Pick the machine it runs on. Swap any device, add a skill, drop a whole team of agents onto the chain as a rack of its own.

The transport becomes the foreman's desk. Play runs the work. Stop pauses at the next safe point and keeps what every agent had in mind; press it twice to cancel. With Steer on, what you type reaches an agent mid-turn, and with it off your words wait as the next task. Arm decides whether agents write directly or propose. Draw a window across a task and nothing is written before you approve the plan or after it ships. Capture records what you do by hand so an agent can learn it from you, and Tap asks every running agent where it has got to, the answers coming back to the dock where your agents gather at their stations like a hive.

Seen as a world, a piece of work is a planet in the making. The task is the world, its agents move in orbit around it, and their helpers ride smaller orbits inside theirs; a whole programme is a system of such worlds, some settled, some still forming. Lay that programme out as a graph and it becomes a map of what waits on what — how you plan the next stretch, and how you find where it is stuck.

When a world is finished, it doesn't hang there. Some finished work becomes a workflow, the same steps ready to run again on new material. Some becomes a loop that studies its own results and runs better next time. And some worlds simply complete: what they made passes on into the places that needed it, and the world itself folds away.

#7. Expressions — the living medium

Expressions is a medium you step into rather than look at. Text, symbols, image, physics and sound share one field. Particles gather into the shapes you give them and move by the forces you set; symbols hold their places or drift, pull on one another or push apart. Beneath the field lies a plate — in three dimensions, a chamber — and as you sweep it you hear the pattern forming on its surface. The pitch rides the lines where the plate stands still, and each symbol can carry a small voice tuned to its own frequency. Sound and image rise from the same moving state, so when one changes, the other changes with it.

You build in scenes. An Expression holds its scenes the way a Live track holds its clips: launch them, loop them, layer one over another, record them. Open a scene and you have both its sides in front of you — the sequence step by step, each symbol in its own lane like a note on a roll, each step's hold and change set like a note's length and glide, and beneath it what the scene sounds like. Add a voice, pin its force, turn the plate on and sweep it until the figure says what you meant. Take the swell, layer the next section over it, capture the figure as a new scene, and save.

What belongs to one scene is in the scene itself, open below the field: its length and what it carries, what follows it, the tools that draw a form, bind a symbol, arrange several or tie six into one figure, and the symbol you have selected, with its place, its force, its voice and its meaning. What shapes the field is in the device area. The studio holds the controls you chose to play, the same in every scene, and the devices that govern the field itself — physics, the pointer, how symbols relate, the medium they move through, what happens when they touch, morph, focus, colour, ink, depth, resonance — in four chains: material, motion, body, and a personal layer that stays yours however the scene is shared. Each is a whole device with every setting it needs. Read one at a glance on its chain, open it as a band above the chain with every setting, let it fill a pane or a window of its own, or keep it as a tile on the rail. Its settings stay live whichever you look at.

The transport turns in this case to the field. Tempo is the speed it forms and breathes at, and the time signature is the scene's ratio of hold to change. Key is the resonance the field is tuned to, which can be taken from the sky over your world so that colours and intervals shift as the planets move. Play plays the scenes. Arm the studio and Play records a take; record several, and keep the one that stands.

Agents can express through this medium as well. An interface built for them lets an agent open an Expression, compose in it and perform it, so that what an agent finds can reach you as something you see and hear. Teaching agents this well is ongoing work. The library of Expressions people make is how they learn: it shows them what an expression is, and how differently two people can give form to the same thing.

Anything in any mode can be given an expression — a day, a run of work, a source, a place, an argument you can't yet put into words — and every mode can open the expression stage beside its own panes. Scenes made here travel: onto a walk, into a palace, into someone else's world. In time Live joins this medium, and sound and picture will share one timeline the way a film editor holds them.

And you can simply stay with it. Turn the lights down, let a single symbol turn in the field and listen to what it does.

#8. Technē — crafting knowledge

Technē is where knowledge is made by building worlds. Find a subject and gather what surrounds it: the people involved, the places, the sources and what they claim. Set it on the Earth where it happened and under the sky it happened beneath, lay its history along the ruler, walk its places in order, and build it a palace someone else can enter. Places, peoples and agents, ways of seeing, the journeys between them: here they are the material itself.

Why build knowledge like this? Because that is how people have always held it. We remember by place — the old art of memory set each thing to be remembered in a room of an imagined building, and a walk through the rooms brought it all back. We understand by time, by seeing what came before and what followed and what happened at once. And we think by relation, by putting one thing next to another and seeing that they belong together. A page of text holds these things flat. Technē gives each its own dimension, so that you can see where an idea lived, when it changed, what it touched, and walk through it in an order that makes sense. Everything you place carries its source and its owner, so a reader can always go back and check. And because each reading of a subject is a layer laid over the others, never a replacement for them, people who disagree can work on the same ground, with the disagreement in plain view. That is a different culture of knowledge from the one we have: shared without being flattened, open to anyone willing to walk it.

Everything starts from the world. Pull back and you see the field of worlds turning around their stars. Come in, and a single body fills the pane, then a region, then one place with its exact position and the frame it was measured in. Turn the world to its graph side and the same material lays out by connection, each thing coloured by what it is, the ties between them showing what joins them. Build your own planet here — its seas and continents, climate and sky, and the places, palaces, roads and portals you set on it. Earth is given; you place things on it, and anchor your own world's places to its real ones.

The canvas is where you think out loud. Throw ideas onto it, drag them into clusters, draw a frame around a group, run a line from one thing to another to say they belong together, and keep going until the shape of the problem shows. It works the way a mind map does, with a difference: everything on it is a real thing you can open, and a line you draw stays a suggestion until whoever owns that relation agrees to it.

The timeline sets history beside the work that studied it, on one ruler that can stretch from deep time to this morning. One person's reading of an age is a layer of tracks, and a later reading lies over it without erasing it. Ages nest inside ages, and the narrowest colours everything inside it. Read the same events by date, by cause, by what keeps recurring, or by what happened at once. Everything keeps the weight it has — fact, claim, inference, guess, dispute, or a gap nobody has filled — and setting two things side by side never turns a coincidence into a cause.

The journey is an ordered walk through places, moments and things, with a pace, a way of arriving at each stop, and a voice that tells it. The palace gives a whole subject a building to live in: rooms that each hold a part of it, a path through them, a guide who explains why each room is there, a private room that stays clouded, and doors out to other places and other people's worlds.

A walk is where all of these meet. Take a thread from the timeline and walk it, and each stop sets the clock to its year and the world to its place, and launches the scenes it carries — an image, a film, a living field. A walk can pass through several Expressions, one scene from each, or step down through the scenes of one. Press Record and go somewhere new; your stops become the walk's stops, and it lays itself along the ruler, ready to edit and ready to give away.

Above the world the sky turns. Stand in today's, or in the sky of any date you choose, and work there; one tap brings you back to now. The transport walks: tempo is the walk's pace, the time signature tells you which stop of how many, quantize decides how each stop arrives. Key is how far you are looking and as whom — your own world, the shared field or beyond; as yourself, as the field, as the public. Beside the instruments works a companion who reads the sources with you, proposes what might follow, and can tell you why any room in a palace stands where it does.

#9. Live — the instrument

At the heart of the DLW is one instrument, and Live mode is where you play it.

It is a torus, turning in the centre of the pane, and what you see is the sound itself: a field of vibration wound around a ring, its surface patterned by what it is doing. Two pulses drive it, beating against each other, and everything you hear comes from them. Touch the surface and it rings. Play a note and a point on the ring lights and answers; play a chord and several points light together, and the figure they draw on the surface is the chord's shape — the same shape the DLW uses for the symbol that chord belongs to. Play a symbol and you hear its harmony. Play its harmony and the symbol appears.

You play it from a keyboard laid out in six rows, two families of whole tones interleaved so that every semitone is a short step across and every chord has the same fingering in every key. Wrap those two rows around the ring and they become the torus's two helices, which is why a key on the keyboard and a point on the torus are the same thing. Use a mouse, your fingers, the computer keyboard or a MIDI controller; or switch to an ordinary piano layout if you would rather.

Choose the mode you are playing in and the keyboard and the piano roll follow it, marking the notes that belong and folding away the ones that don't. The modes reach from the plain diatonic scale to the full set the logic generates, and beside them sit older scales that keep their own intervals and their own history: the planetary scales Marsilio Ficino composed in, meant to draw each planet's quality into whoever heard them; the Shem scales; the maqām. Over the instrument turns the sky of your world, and each planet can move a part of the sound — its own interval, its own colour — shown on the control it is moving.

The mode lives where Live keeps its scales. Set it in the transport and the whole set plays in it: root, mode and tuning, with the keyboard recoloured to match. Open a clip and it can carry a mode of its own, so a piece can move from one to another phrase by phrase, and the roll folds to that clip's notes or highlights them in place. In the chain, devices work on the notes before they reach the torus — one bends whatever you play onto the mode, one sets the tuning in exact ratios, one hands parts of the sound to the planets overhead — and you can stack, reorder and save them like any other.

Shape the sound as far as you like. Every part of the instrument is a setting you can turn, record and save, and a saved state is a sound you can return to, share or start from. Put devices on its chain to filter it, echo it, give it a room to sound in. Record what you play, edit it note by note in the roll, layer it, loop it, arrange it into a piece.

The same torus turns at the centre of every Expression, so what you play here can be heard and seen there, inside a scene. One day the two will share a timeline, picture and sound together.

#10. Working the window

Everything in this section is the same in every kind of work. Learn it once.

#10.1 The window

The window has five parts, and each one has one job. The transport across the top runs things. The browser down the left holds what you can bring in. Your work fills the middle. The widget rail on the right keeps what you want at hand. The detail area along the bottom shows what is selected and what is working on it.

Part What it is for
Transport Start, stop, record and set the pace of whatever you are working on. Choose the kind of work and the view.
Browser Find material, instruments and devices, and bring them in.
Panes Hold your work. Each pane holds one instrument.
Widget rail Keep context at hand in every kind of work: the agent you are talking to, the packet, the trail, a terminal, the sky.
Detail See the selected clip, and the chain of devices that works on it, one above the other.
Status bar Read what any control does. Show Clip view, Device view, or both.

To hide a part, click its toggle. The other parts grow to fill the space.

#10.2 Panes and layouts

Panes let you keep several things in front of you at once: a document beside the day it belongs to, an agent's thread beside the files it is changing, the timeline beside the place it describes.

To open an instrument, drag it from the browser into a pane. You can also click it, and it opens in the pane that has focus. To split a pane, click ⊞. Then select Beside, Below or Four ways. To move a tab, drag it onto the centre of another pane. To make a new split, drag a tab onto the edge of a pane.

A pane follows what you select, unless you pin it. A pinned pane keeps its subject while you work elsewhere.

Some instruments come in more than one build, the way Live has more than one reverb. Each build answers to the same controls, so you can change the build without losing your settings.

A layout is an arrangement of panes, instruments, widgets and detail. Each kind of work opens with its own layout, and the workspace you are in remembers which. To keep an arrangement, keep it into a workspace in the pool.

#10.3 The browser and the pool

The browser holds four kinds of thing: material to open, instruments to open in a pane, devices to put on a chain, and running things you can work with where they are.

Preview, open and add are three separate actions. To preview something, select it once: its preview shows at the foot of the list, where Live plays a sample you select. To open it, double-click it or drag it into a pane. To add a device, drag it onto a chain. To seat an agent, drag it onto an empty track.

Under the browser is the pool, where Live keeps its Groove Pool. A groove shapes how a clip plays without being part of the clip, and the pool holds what shapes how you work without being the work itself: your workspaces. A workspace is a way of working on something, kept: which panes are open and what they hold, which instruments and builds, which widgets on the rail, and the layout each mode opens with. Launch one and all of it comes back. Drop what is in front of you onto the pool and it becomes a new workspace, the way a clip dropped on Live's pool gives up its groove. In Live the pool also holds the set's grooves, as Live's does.

The pool shows nothing else. A device opens whole in its band above the chain, and an instrument opens in a pane.

#10.4 Devices

A device does one job to the material in front of it. An instrument makes something new: an agent answers, the field moves, the torus rings. An effect changes what passes through it and keeps the rest: a highlight marks a passage and leaves the passage whole. Devices sit left to right in a chain, and the material always passes through them in that order.

A rack holds several chains side by side under one device, with a selector to choose between them. An agent's skills sit in a rack, and so do the agents of a team. A macro is one knob that turns several settings at once, each within its own range.

Every device has six views, and all six show the same live settings:

View Where What you get
Folded Your chain, along the bottom The device's name and a reading or two, in a narrow strip
Compact Your chain A small live picture and up to four controls
Whole Expanded above the chain Every setting, grouped, round a larger picture
Full A pane The device's material at full size, to work in
Pop-out Its own window, which can fill the screen The material with every setting under it
Widget The rail on the right A small picture and a few readings, in every mode

The head opens a device each way. ▸ beside its light shows its expanded view, a band above the chain, as Live's does; ▦ opens it in a pane, and ↗ pops it out into a window, as Live opens a plug-in. Double-click the head to fold the device to its title. Select a device in the browser to read what it does; drag it onto a chain to use it.

A light on a device is a reading. It is on only when the device reports that it is on. If the state is not known, the light stays dark.

When something moves a value, the value shows what moved it. An agent's edit carries the agent's name. A knob that the sky is turning shows the planet that turns it. If you take hold of something an agent or instrument was holding, the hand-back light comes on. Press it to give control back.

You can build new devices that sit in the same chain, as Max for Live devices do in Live. Each part of the suite brings its own devices: the day and its documents, the agents and what tunes them, the skills they carry, the work they do, the machines they run on, and the sky and its clock.

#10.5 The transport

The transport keeps Live's controls in Live's places in every kind of work. What each control does follows the work in front of you. The table shows each control in each mode.

The day opens and closes by itself. No hours are set in the instrument.

#10.6 Widgets

The rail on the right holds widgets: small live views of whatever you want to keep at hand while you work. The agent you are talking to is one. Others hold the packet, the trail, your agents at their stations, a terminal, the files an agent is changing, the changes waiting for your approval, the wiki, a calendar, the sky. Each widget holds a piece of context that belongs to your whole world, not to one kind of work, so it stays with you when you move. Open a terminal in Factory and it is still there in Central.

To open a widget, click its square in the launcher at the bottom right. To stop a widget following your selection, pin it. To widen the rail, drag its edge. To keep a group of widgets, save it as a preset.

Widgets are plugins. You can set each one up the way your work needs, and anyone can build new ones. To add a widget, install it from the browser.

#10.7 Going back

Every place you go is kept on the trail. To go back, select a stop on the trail. You return to the same place, the same page and the same point in the reading. Changing your kind of work never closes anything, and every place can be linked to and opened again.

#Part Two — The Game

#11. The figure

The game began, Hesse tells us, on a frame like a child's abacus. A musicologist strung glass beads of different sizes and colours along its wires, and the wires stood for the lines of a stave, so that he could set out a theme in beads, transpose it, develop it, and play one theme against another. Within a few generations the beads were gone. What remained was a language in which mathematics and music counted equally, where a Bach fugue, a law of nature and a saying of Confucius could stand in the same sentence. Hesse's narrator calls it "a mode of playing with the total contents and values of our culture," and says its player plays "like the organist on an organ."

Hesse wrote around the rules rather than writing them down, but he lets us watch a game closely enough to follow it. A master sets a symbol before the players. Each of them stops and considers it in silence — what it holds, where it came from, what it means — so that the sign will not go empty. Then the theme is worked against others, the way a fugue works its subject. New symbols enter the game's archive rarely, through one competition a year. Great games run for days, under rules strict enough to say how long the players may sleep, and private games are played by anyone who wants to play one. Every game is written down so that it can be read back. And the master who has given his life to it knows that it will end; beauty, once it has entered history, passes.

Nobody built it. For most of a century the Glass Bead Game was the closest picture we had of a whole environment for meaning, and it stayed a picture. Then music, the art Hesse had taken for his model, got the environment he imagined. Live put composition, sound design, recording, mixing and performance in one place with one clock, and anyone could sit down at it.

The DLW takes the step from there to what Hesse described: Live's grammar, pointed at the contents of a world. Its centre is an instrument, as it should be. Hesse's game grew from a bead frame strung like a stave. The DLW grows from a torus whose keys are points along its helices, and whose chords draw the symbols they belong to. Here, as in Hesse's novel, the language of symbols and the language of sound are one language.

#12. The game's language

In Hesse's game the player was never only moving symbols about. Each theme was a piece of the world's knowledge, and each move worked on the player as well. Knecht describes a game well played closing around the one who played it the way a sphere closes around its centre, so that they came away having taken a whole, ordered world into themselves. The DLW is built for that double movement. What you make in your world becomes knowledge others can walk through, and making it is how you come to know. Take either half away and the other goes hollow.

You develop a theme by carrying it. You meet it in the day's work, take it into a scene until you can hear what it is, and lay it along a walk that someone else can follow. Each step changes its form, and each step asks something different of you.

Hesse's players knew two schools. One found the game's unity in the material, in how the themes fitted together. The other found it in the player, in a sequence of meditations that led them, move by move, towards experiencing the whole. One school faces the world and the other faces the self, and the DLW keeps room for both. In Technē you work the material until it holds: the history checked, the relations accepted, the palace built room by room. In Expressions you meet the same material as something lived, a field you stand inside and listen to.

At the great public games, what the people watching valued most was the silence. After each symbol every player stopped and considered it — what it held, where it came from, what it meant — so that the sign would stay full. The DLW gives that silence its place in Expressions, where a presence meets you in the field, listens, answers, and never judges. Whether the instrument should ever require the pause, as Hesse's game did, is left open. Nothing automatic is allowed to fill it in the meantime.

Any game could be read back into the disciplines it was made from. Knecht spent years translating a single school game, phrase by phrase, into mathematics, ornament, Chinese and Greek. Every reference in the DLW keeps its owner and its version so that you can do the same: follow anything back to where it began. Jean Gebser called that beginning the ever-present origin, a source that stays present rather than receding into the past. Tracing it is a kind of care, and it runs in both directions: back through what a thing came from, and back through what you came from in coming to know it.

Magister Ludi first meant simply schoolmaster, and Knecht ends his life as a teacher. The DLW teaches the way any instrument does. A companion works beside you and explains what you are looking at, and the rest you learn by playing.

Hesse gave his players a village. The DLW gives them a field. Every player is a world in it, every visit lands on what its host has chosen to show, and every gift arrives under its giver's name. He kept the private game as well — "to this day everyone is free to play the Game privately" — and your world, before you have shown any of it, is where that game is played.

Games were written down so they could be played again. Here a day closes into what it taught, a walk is recorded, a take is kept, and whoever you give them to can play them in their own world.

And games end. A day in the DLW closes by itself. Whether a whole kind of work, or the instrument itself, might one day be retired with its record kept is a question the DLW keeps open. An instrument that cannot imagine its end will defend it.

#13. Epi-Logos

In Hesse's novel the game belonged to an Order. Scholars who had given their lives to it played it in a closed province, and everyone else came only as guests. Epi-Logos is that game opened to anyone: the Glass Bead Game played with what we now know, by whoever sits down to play.

Take one move. Pythagoras taught that all is number. The Gospel of John opens with the Word. Much later a psychologist and a physicist, Jung and Pauli, wrote to each other for a quarter of a century and came to think that number was the oldest ordering of all, older than the split between mind and matter. Set the three side by side and none of them has to give way: the number that orders, the word that names, and the place the two scientists found where they meet. A player who can hold all three and show how each answers the others has made a move. Hesse's players made the same kind of move with a fugue and a law of nature.

Moves are played on a map. Picture Spinoza's Ethics, set out in geometric order with every proof citing the proofs it stands on, and then picture it alive, so that you can question it. Each idea has an address, each relation a path you can walk, each claim a way back to its source, and everyone who plays adds to it. Most maps stand outside the land they show. This one maps awareness, and nobody can stand outside that, so the map holds the person reading it. You are on it.

The game carries a story about language. Words once acted on the world: a name could call, a spell could bind. Each age since has made words more exact and less alive, until in ours they mostly label what instruments measure. The story ends where we are now, with machines built out of language, and asks whether they could help bring back what was lost — the sense of living inside what we name.

So players dig into words. Every word holds a record of how people have tried to name what is real. Whole, health and holy grow from one root, and you can follow that root down until the three stand together again.

Jung and Pauli also dreamed of a clock of the world, one that would tell the state of meaning rather than the hour. The sky over your planet is the game's attempt to build it. The planets stand where they really stand, and the torus at the centre turns with them. On a day you can't account for, look up, and see where everything stood while you lived it.

None of it counts unless it changes the player. Jung called that change individuation: becoming who you already are. The philosophers of Kashmir called it recognition: finding that what you are was never missing. One path runs inward and the other outward, and Epi-Logos wagers that they meet. A child's taunt says it best. It takes one to know one.

That change is practised in Nara, a world of your own that nobody else can see into. Say a decision has stayed with you for a month. On the first morning you opened it as a journey and drew three cards. Since then you have written beside them, dreamt twice about the house in question, and sat with it most evenings. Nara has read all of it as you wrote it. Ask it now and it answers from those pages, naming each passage it used. The cards are still where they fell, and the I Ching shows how the question has turned.

The machines have work in the game too. They are made of words carried on numbers, so they can read back over everything we have written and help find what it means. They read, gather, propose and build, and they defer — to what they cannot know, and to the person they work for. An agent can hand a decision up. It cannot approve its own work.

The Truth's as much what ever was As what's ever anew.

#14. What the game warns

Hesse wrote the game's decline into its history, and three of his warnings apply to anything built in its image.

A form can grow more beautiful than what it carries, and then it is glass. The DLW meets that by asking of every part what work it does in a real day: whatever cannot do its part is ornament.

A device that judges for the player — scores the scene, picks the reading — takes the silence away. The DLW's companions answer and explain; you decide.

Analysis can outlive the music it studied. The close study of Live serves an instrument that sounds, or it has lost its point.

The test of the whole is what people leave with: a day closed into what it taught, work landed, an expression shown, a reader who understood, a pupil who no longer needs the teacher. Hesse's novel ends with its Magister leaving the Order to teach a single pupil.

#15. Still to decide

  • What the units of time beyond the day are — a week, a season, a year — and how they close.
  • Whether Epii's teaching has a surface of its own or runs through every kind of work.
  • Whether, and where, the instrument should require the pause Hesse's game required.
  • Whether a kind of work, or the instrument itself, can be retired with its record kept.
  • Whether the cycle of the word maps onto the kinds of work at all.
  • How someone who is not the corpus's author enters the corpus itself, and what its discipline asks of them.

#Part Three — The catalogue

#16. How devices work

You play a device while your attention is on the material. The agent runs, the field moves, the torus rings, and your eyes are there, not on the device. So a device must show its state at a glance, answer the hand at once, and never ask you to read. Live solved this. Every Live device is one panel of one height: a picture of what the device does, with handles on it, and the controls set round the picture. You learn one panel and you can read all of them. The DLW draws every device the same way.

Live has one kind of object in its chains, and so does the DLW, in the same three kinds. An instrument makes something. An agent answers, the field moves and sounds, the world draws its places, the timeline lays out time, the Day gathers the day, the torus rings. An effect changes what passes through it and keeps the rest: a scope limits what an agent may touch, a filter shapes the torus, the packet gathers what an agent will read. A rack holds several chains under one device: the skills an agent may use, the agents of a team, the field devices of the Studio.

#16.1 What goes where

A device is one kind of thing among five, and most of what the DLW does belongs to one of the other four. Live draws the lines, and the DLW keeps them.

Thing What it is What belongs to it
Track One stream of material, and the chain that works on it. Its name, colour, the I/O of its work, its mixer row, and its chain. In Central a world; in Factory a session; in Expressions an Expression; in Technē a reading; in Live a part.
Clip A piece of the material on a track. Its name, colour and length, how it launches and what follows it, and its content. Everything that changes the clip itself belongs here: a highlight on a page, a symbol's place in a scene, a task's instruction.
Clip view The clip's editor, in the detail area. Panels after Live's: Clip, Launch, the editor, and Envelopes. What each mode's editor holds is in §16.5.
Device Works on the material as it passes along the chain. What changes how the material sounds, looks or acts, without rewriting the clip, for every clip on the track.
View A way of seeing tracks and clips. Session view's grid, Arrangement view's ruler with its lanes and takes, the panes, the widget rail.

So a capability lives in one place:

  • A thing that edits the clip is a tool of its editor. Text, Highlight and Notes shape and mark the page you are writing. They are the editing tools of a Central page in Clip view, as Live's note tools are, and the passage acts in the page reach them. A selected symbol's force, form, voice and meaning are the selected object's controls in the scene's editor.
  • One job is one device, in every mode. The agent that answers in a Central page and the agent that runs a Factory task is one Agent (§19.4): who it is, how it works, how much it decides alone, and its thread. What an agent may read and where it may write is one Scope (§19.12), with the Knowledge route inside it, on any track an agent works on.
  • How a write arrives is set in two places only. Scope sets the most an agent may do in each part of the world: propose, draft or write. Arm, in the transport, lets the agents of a track write directly with receipts or keeps them proposing. Every proposal waits in Approvals on the Main track, and in the page or scene it belongs to.
  • A widget is a view of a device. The ledger, the approvals, the packet, the terminal and the frames on the rail are the widget views of their devices. A widget with no device elsewhere, such as Files or Calendar, is a small device born on the rail.
  • The views are not devices. Session view and Arrangement view hold the tracks of every mode, and a pane can show either for any set of tracks. The course of a run is its track in Arrangement view; its attempts are takes; a task is a clip.
  • A corpus is material, not an instrument. A body of work such as the Return of Zero or the Quaternal Logic is read, walked and played through the universal instruments: the world, the timeline, the journey, the palace, the graph. A device a corpus brings, such as the Deck or the M3 clock, is a lens on its material, and sits on a reading's chain like any other.

#16.2 Chains on tracks

A track holds one stream of material. Its chain holds the devices that the material passes through, from left to right.

Mode Track Chain, left to right
Central A world. The set is the Day; its die is on the Main track. The packet, the agent of the world, and its scope; on a world whose folder holds code, Ground hygiene and Impact. On the Main track: the Day, then Approvals.
Factory An agent session. Gateway, agent, skills rack, scope, landing. On the Main track: the gateways rack, the experiments, Approvals, the ledger and Link.
Expressions An Expression. The Studio: the field, then its field devices.
Technē A reading. The instrument it plays through, then its lens and filter effects.
Live A part. The note devices, the torus, then its effects.

A device is not tied to a mode. It goes wherever its material is. An agent and its skills work on a Central page as well as on a Factory session. The world draws any track that has places. The sky can move a parameter in any mode.

#16.3 Six views

A device has one set of values and six views. A change in one view shows in the others at once.

View Where What it holds Live's nearest
Folded The chain The activator, the name and up to two short readings, read downward, in a strip 22 px wide. A folded device.
Compact The chain The display and up to four macros, at the chain's height. The width comes from the sections. The device on its chain; a rack with only its macros showing.
Whole Expanded above the chain Every parameter, grouped, and the display with all its handles, in a band as wide as the detail area: the bands stand side by side and the display grows into the room. The expanded view of EQ Eight or Wavetable.
Full A pane The material itself: the page, the field, the torus, the world. No controls. Wavetable's large display, without its controls.
Pop-out Its own window, which can fill the screen The material with every control docked under it. A plug-in's window.
Widget The rail A small display and up to three readings or macros, in a tile of 136 × 136 px or 280 × 136 px. Live has none. Push's display is the nearest.
  • Whole stands in one place, its band above the chain. A device you have not put on a chain has no Whole: select it in the browser to read what it does.
  • Most devices need no pop-out of their own. The window shows Full above and Whole below it. A device writes its own only where its controls belong beside the material rather than under it.
  • A pane holds no device controls. They stay in Device view under the pane, or in the device's window.

#16.4 Opening, folding and hiding

The window is modular: every part opens, folds and hides, and the other parts take the space.

A device's head reads as Live's does, from the left:

Part Does
Activator Asks the owner to turn the device on or off. The light shows what the owner reports.
▸ Expanded view Shows the device's expanded view: Whole, as a band above the chain. ▾ while it stands; press it again to hide it.
Name Double-click the head to fold the device to its title, or choose Fold from its context menu. Press the folded title to open it again.
Rail, ▦ Pane, ↗ Pop out At the right. Keep the device on the widget rail; open it full in the pane that has focus; open it in its own window. Press ↗ in the window to close it.

Press the plate to select the device: the keys and the status bar speak to it. Right-click the head for Fold, Rename, Group, Save as preset, Hot-swap, Show automation and Map. A rack's head has three more marks, as Live's do: show or hide the macros, the chain list, and the devices of the selected chain.

The expanded view is a band as wide as the detail area, between Clip view and the chain. The panes above it shrink to make room; nothing is covered. One device stands expanded at a time, and it stays on the chain under its band. Any number of windows can stay open. A window follows its device: when the device leaves the chain, its window closes.

The detail area holds Clip view above Device view, both at once when there is room, as Live 12 does. Hide either one, or switch between them when only one shows.

To show or hide Do
The detail area ⌘⌥L, or its toggle in the status bar.
Clip view or Device view ⇧Tab, or the switches in the status bar.
Clip view in the main area ⌥⌘E. The clip's editor takes the space of the panes; press again to return.
The browser ⌘⌥B.
The pool ⌥⌘6, as Live shows its Groove Pool. Drag the line between the browser and the pool to give either the height.
The widget rail ⌘⌥R. Drag its edge to widen it. Fold the launcher to give the tiles the height.
A device's expanded view ▸ on its head, or Esc.
A device's window ↗ on its head, or the window's own close.

#16.5 Clip view and Device view

Select a clip and the detail area shows it. Clip view shows the clip and its editor. Device view shows the chain of the clip's track.

Clip view is laid out as Live lays it out, in every mode. Only what each part holds changes with the kind of clip.

Part Where What it holds Live's
Head Across the top The clip's activator, its name on its colour. The clip's title bar.
Clip The first panel, first tab Where the clip starts and ends, whether it loops, its length, its signature and its scale, and the facts that belong to the clip alone. Start, End, Loop, Position, Length, Signature, Groove, Scale.
Launch The first panel, second tab How the clip launches, when the launch lands, and what follows it, with what chance. Launch mode, Legato, Quantize, Velocity, Follow Action.
Tools The second panel, in tabs Tools that change the clip's content as a whole. A transform reshapes what is there; a generator proposes new content. A tool shows its few controls and one button that applies it, or applies as you turn it. Pitch and Time, Transform, Generate.
Editor The rest of the area, in tabs The content itself. The first tab is the clip's own material. Envelopes moves a parameter over the clip's own time. A third tab holds what each element of the content carries on its own, lane by lane. Notes or Sample, Envelopes, MPE.
  • The editor folds and highlights as Live's does: Fold keeps only the rows that hold content, and a scale or a set of kinds highlights what belongs.
  • An envelope picks its device and its parameter. On a device's parameter it is Automation, which sets the value over the clip, or Modulation, which moves the track's own value up and down. Linked, it runs with the clip; unlinked, it has its own length and loop.
  • A clip on Arrangement view's ruler has no Launch tab, as in Live. A clip that is placed rather than launched, such as a Central page, never has one.
  • A change in the editor changes the clip. A device on the chain changes what passes through it and leaves the clip as it is (§16.1).
  • What a clip lacks, Clip view leaves out.
Mode The clip Its editor's tabs Launch Envelopes
Technē (§17.1) An event or a walk's stop Material, Relations, Constellation, Source Arrival, and the walk's next stop None
Central (§18.1) A page Page and Source; a Flow's Dialogue, Flow and Journal None None
Factory (§19.1) A task The log Mode, Then, Quantize The Agent's Effort and Autonomy for this task
Expressions (§20.1) A scene Sequence, Envelopes, Expression, Sound Quantize, the next scene, Jump The scene's automation
Live (§21.1) A note clip or an audio clip Notes, Envelopes, MPE; or Sample, Envelopes Live's own Live's own

#16.6 Tracks, Session view and Arrangement view

Every mode draws its tracks the same way, and a pane can show either view for any set of tracks.

Mode Track Clip Stop (a row) Takes
Central A world. The set is the Day; its die is on the Main track. A page: a being, a thing or a flow. Pages you open together. Revisions: draft, saved, edited since save.
Factory An agent session. A task. A batch of tasks that launch together. Attempts. You choose the attempt that lands.
Expressions An Expression. A scene. A moment across several Expressions. Captured takes of a scene.
Technē A reading, a journey or the occurrences of a subject. An event, an occurrence or a revision at its time and place. A stop of a walk: it stands its event and launches the scenes it carries. Readings that extend a reading.
Live A part. A note clip or an audio clip. A row of clips. Take lanes.

The word scene always means an Expression's scene. A row of clips is a stop.

#The track

A track's head shows its colour, its name and, in Factory, its agent. Groups fold under one header; the members of a group step in below it, with one coloured rail for each level, and a row in a group launches all its children at once.

Under the head is the track's mixer row, after Live's. What each control means follows the mode; the mode's own section says how (§17.1–§21.1).

Control Live's meaning
Activator The track's work reaches the Main track, or runs silent.
Solo Only the soloed tracks run, or show.
Arm The track records, and its agent writes directly, with receipts.
Monitor In, Auto or Off: when you watch the track's input.
Input and output Where the track's material comes from, and where it goes: to the Main track by default.

The Main track sits at the end, where Live keeps it. It carries each row's launch button, Stop All, and Back to Arrangement, which lights when what plays differs from the ruler. Its own chain holds what works on the whole: the Day and Approvals in Central; the gateways, the experiments, Approvals, the ledger and Link in Factory.

#Session view

Session view is the grid: tracks across, rows down, everything launchable, nothing in a fixed order.

A clip that is Looks
Waiting A fast blink. It has no length, because nobody knows how long it will take.
Running A slow blink and a fill that grows. It has no end drawn.
Stopped Ready, in full colour.
Recording Marked with the record light.
Stalled Striped and frozen where its output stopped. The track lights a retry.
Done Dimmed and settled.

An empty slot is a place to write: double-click it to start a clip there. Each track has a stop button in its empty slots. In Factory, a track whose clip has stalled lights a retry, which starts again from the last good step on the same basis. How a clip launches, when it lands and what follows it are the clip's own, in its Launch tab. The transport's Quantize is the launch quantize for every clip set to Global. The number keys launch rows.

#Arrangement view

Arrangement view lays the same tracks along one ruler.

Mode The ruler measures
Central Today, from first entry to last.
Factory The time of the run. Each session has a needle at its last check-in.
Expressions The scenes in order, in seconds, with each Expression's picture lane over its sound lane.
Technē Time from deep time to today, with readings as layers of tracks.
Live Bars and beats.

Under each track lie its lanes:

  • Automation lanes, one for each parameter that moves: an agent's effort over a run, a field device over a scene, the sky's pull on a filter. Each names its device and its parameter. Arm the track and move the parameter, and the move becomes a lane. A modulated parameter shows what moves it. A value you override gives control back only when you press Re-enable.
  • Take lanes, one for each take, as Live's are. Audition a take from its lane. Select a stretch of a take and press Return to copy it into the main lane: that is how you choose the attempt that lands, the capture that stands, or the revision you keep. The main lane shows which take each stretch came from.
  • Event lanes, in Factory: each session's messages, files, terminal and pages read. Click an event and it opens beside the ruler.

A needle jumps each time its agent checks in, and a needle that does not move marks a stall: the thread freezes where its output stopped. A line marks now, and Follow keeps it in view.

Named sections run along the top; in Technē they are epochs, they nest, and the narrowest colours the clips inside it. A marker is a named, coloured point. A loop region has an in point and an out point: the transport plays, and repeats, only between them. In Factory the same brace is the window in which agents may write.

A clip stays the same thing wherever you move it. A move changes only where it stands in time.

Edit What it does
Move, insert Moves the clip and shifts the clips after it.
Move, overwrite Moves the clip and replaces what was there.
Trim Drags an edge to change the length.
Roll Moves the seam between two clips. One grows by what the other loses.
Slip Changes where the clip starts inside its own content.
Slide Moves a clip between fixed neighbours.
Split Cuts the clip in two at the playhead. The lengths add up to the original.

A void is a still clip, not a gap. Every edit is one labelled step in the undo list; a split or a roll is one step, not three. Snap pulls the playhead and the edges you drag to clip edges, markers, loop edges and the playhead; hold Alt to turn it off while you drag.

Setting Values Default What it does
Tracks shown Any set of the mode's tracks All The tracks in view.
Lanes Takes, Automation, Events, each On · Off Takes On, Automation On, Events Off The lanes under the tracks.
Time scale Even · Stretched Even Stretched keeps deep time readable beside today.
Zoom Any span Fit all The span in view.
Snap On · Off On Aligns dragged items to edges, markers and the playhead.
Move mode Insert · Overwrite Insert What a move does to its neighbours.
Trim mode Trim · Roll · Slip · Slide Trim What an edge drag does.
Overview strip On · Off On The whole ruler, with the view marked.
Standing floor Facts only · Plus claims · Plus inferences · Plus guesses · All All In Technē, hides clips that stand below the level.

#Rules

  • A track holds the material of its owner. The views show it and never copy it.
  • A clip that is running shows no end. A waiting clip shows no length. A stalled clip shows where it stopped and does not look done.
  • A row launches only what is in it. Stop pressed twice cancels at once; launch quantize does not delay it.
  • A move does not change what a clip is. Placement does not raise a claim to a fact.
  • Every edit is one labelled step that can be undone alone.
  • Every mode uses the same states, the same stop buttons and the same keys.

#16.7 The browser and the pool

In every mode the browser holds four kinds of thing. Material comes from the mode's owners: worlds, their beings, things and flows, and past days in Central; sessions in Factory; scenes in Expressions; sources in Technē; sets in Live. Instruments, devices and widgets are the same in every mode, grouped by the product that makes them. Select a row to preview it at the foot of the list: a page's first lines, a material's picture, a device's account of what it does. Drag a device onto a chain to add it. Drag an instrument into a pane to open it.

#The pool

The pool stands under the browser in every mode, where Live keeps its Groove Pool, and holds your workspaces. A workspace keeps a way of working; it never holds the work. In Live a switch at the pool's head shows the set's grooves instead, as Live's pool does.

Column Values What it does
Workspace A name, on its colour The workspace. The one you are in is lit.
Panes On · Off Keeps the panes, how they are split and pinned, and what each holds.
Instruments On · Off Keeps the instrument in each pane and its build.
Widgets On · Off Keeps the widgets on the rail and their presets.
Layouts On · Off Keeps the layout each mode opens with.
Opens in Last mode · Central · Factory · Expressions · Technē · Live The mode that comes forward after a launch.

Each row has two acts, as a groove has hot-swap and Commit:

Act What it does
▶ Launch Brings the workspace's context back, in the modes the footer says.
Keep Stores what is in front of you now into the workspace.

The footer stands where Live's Global Amount does:

Control Values Default What it does
On launch Restore all modes · Restore open mode · Restore nothing Restore all modes What comes back when you launch a workspace.
Keep context On change · On leave · Manual On change When the workspace you are in stores your context.

Drop a pane's tab onto the pool and the arrangement around it becomes a new workspace. Drag a workspace onto a world's track to make it that world's way of working: opening the world's pages brings it forward.

In Live the pool's other face is Live's own: Groove, Base, Quantize, Timing, Random and Velocity for each of the set's grooves, Global Amount at the foot, and "Drop clips or grooves here" when it is empty. Each groove is the set's, read from it and written into it.

  • The pool holds workspaces and, in Live, grooves. It holds no device, no instrument and no pane's contents.
  • A workspace stores context. The pages and files stay where they are.
  • Launching a workspace changes no page, no world and no Day.
  • A layout you keep belongs to the workspace you are in.

#16.8 Play, record and automation

Play launches clips. Each clip's track runs through its chain, and the instrument makes its part: the agent runs, the field plays, the walk walks. Record lays what played into Arrangement view as clips, with takes under them.

Arm, then move any parameter of any device, and the move is recorded as an automation lane on that track. You can automate an agent's effort over a run, the pace of a walk, a field device over a scene, the sky's pull on a filter. Arrangement view shows each track's clips, and under them the lanes of its devices. A clip can carry envelopes of its own, which move a parameter over the clip's own time. Re-enable gives a parameter back to its lane, or to the agent or instrument that held it. A modulated parameter shows what moves it.

#16.9 How a device looks

A designed face, not a form. A device's parameters are its contract with its owner. Its panel is drawn by hand for that device, as every Live device is. A list of parameters drawn one under the other is the fallback, and only for a device that ships no panel of its own. Live does the same for a plug-in that has no editor.

The picture comes first. Most panels have a display: a dark well that draws the device's state from its live values. EQ Eight draws its curve, Auto Filter its filter, Wavetable its wave. Handles in the well are controls: drag the picture and the parameter moves. The other controls stand round the display, in sections.

One height, many widths. On a chain, every device has the same height. The width comes from what the device holds. A small effect is narrow, and an instrument with two displays is wide.

Nothing scrolls, and no box sits inside a box. Sections are columns, ruled off from each other by a hairline and named in small capitals. The well is the only inset surface. When a panel holds more than fits, it gets pages in its head, as Wavetable has Osc 1 and Osc 2.

Values are short. A control shows a number, a unit or a word. A sentence never appears in a panel. What a control does is said in the status bar when you point at it.

#Measures

All measures are in pixels at 100 %. The grid is 4 px.

Part Measure
Head 22 high
Band (the body on a chain) 168 high
Plate on a chain 191 high: head, band and borders
Control column 56 wide
Dial 28 square, a 270° ring
Value box 16 high, 50 wide or more
Toggle, radio button, chooser, action 16 high
Hit area of any control 22 or more in each direction
Row of a list 17 high
Display on a compact plate 140 × 146
Whole expanded The detail area's width. One band high, its bands side by side; a display may grow the band up to half the height of the panes.
Pop-out window 1180 × 640 when it first opens, then the size you leave it at. Whole is docked under the material, one band high.
Section label 9 px capitals, 13 high
Names and values 10 to 11 px
Folded strip 22 wide
Widget tile 136 × 136, or 280 × 136

The plate does not reflow. When the detail area is shorter or taller, the whole plate scales between 80 % and 125 %, as Live's zoom scales its devices.

#The head

From left to right:

  1. Activator. A round light. Amber: the owner reports the device on. Grey: the owner reports it off. A hollow ring: the owner has not said. Press it to ask the owner to turn the device on or off. The light changes only when the owner answers.
  2. Expanded view ▸, Live's toggle. Double-click the head to fold the device to its title.
  3. Name. A rack shows its set's name: "Skills · build-lane".
  4. Held mark. The name of whoever holds values on this device, in the stall colour.
  5. Pages. One tab for each page. The shown page joins the body's face. A page can carry its own light, as Hybrid Reverb's EQ tab does.
  6. The device's own acts, such as the Studio's Save and Undo.
  7. Rail, ▦ Pane, ↗ Pop out, at the right (§16.4).

Right-click the head for Save as preset, Hot-swap, Show automation and Map.

#The body

The body is one band on a chain. Whole, expanded or in a window, sets its bands side by side. A band is a row of sections. A section is a column of controls with a label. The display stands in a section of its own, at the left.

#Colours

Colour Means
Face The body of the plate.
Head Lighter than the face. Tinted blue when the device is selected.
Field The ground of value boxes, toggles and choosers.
Well The display. Near black.
Teal A value: a dial's arc, a value box's fill, a curve, a reach for reading.
Amber On: a toggle, a chosen radio button, the activator, a handle, a reach for writing.
Green An owner's yes: a skill admitted, a task done.
Orange Someone else holds this value.
Red Automation, and the record button.

A device does not bring colours of its own. A colour inside a display carries a meaning of the material, as a track's colour does.

#16.10 Controls and the hand

A panel uses these controls and no others.

Control Use it for It shows Live's nearest
Dial A number that you sweep The name above, a 270° ring with a teal arc, the value below. A value that can turn either way starts its arc at zero. A knob.
Value box A number where space is short, or one you set exactly The value in a field that fills teal to its place in the range. A slider, such as Wavetable's Volume.
Toggle On or off The name in a box, amber when on. Auto Filter's Hz button.
Radio One of two to five Buttons side by side or stacked; the chosen one amber. A choice the owner cannot take stands dimmed, and the status bar says why. Auto Filter's filter types.
Chooser One of many The value and ▾; a menu. A closed choice is dimmed in the menu, with its reason. Wavetable's wavetable menu.
Swatch A colour The colour in a small square, then its value. Press it to pick another. A clip's or a track's colour.
Rows A list: a rack's chains, a scope's places One row each: your request as a square; the owner's answer as a light, or the member's kind mark in its state; the name; a short meter; a short note. A long list pages. Where the order is yours, the chosen row carries ▲ ▼, and ⌥↑ ⌥↓ move it. A rack's chain list.
Ladder Evidence in steps One tick for each step: lit where it is established, dark where it is not, hollow where nobody has observed it. A level meter's segments.
Readout A reading A short value over its name. A large readout is the count that the view is for. Hybrid Reverb's "100 % / 285 ms".
Action Something done once The name in an outlined box. A momentary button.
Light A state that the owner reports A small round light. A chain's activator.
Handle A parameter that you move on the picture An amber ring, numbered in order of importance. EQ Eight's band dots.

The same moves work on every control that holds a number.

Do Result
Drag up or down The value moves. The whole range is 160 px. A stepped value moves one step for each 14 px.
Hold Shift while you drag The value moves ten times more slowly.
Double-click The value returns to its default.
Focus it and press ↑ ↓ or ← → One step, or 1 % of the range.
Page Up, Page Down 10 % of the range.
Home, End The minimum, the maximum.
Delete The default.
Point at it The status bar says its name and what it does.

A handle moves in the directions that its picture shows. A handle for two parameters, such as gravity across and up, moves in two directions. The arrow keys move a focused handle 2 px, or 0.5 px with Shift.

#A change and its answer

While you drag, the control shows the value you set. When you release it, the new value goes to the owner; a key press sends at once. Until the owner confirms the value, it shows in italics. If the owner refuses, the control returns to the owner's value, and the status bar gives the owner's reason.

Some changes ask you first. When you raise a scope's reach, the new reach waits, drawn dashed, with Confirm and Cancel beside it. Nothing is sent until you confirm.

#16.11 Standing

Besides its value, a control shows who moves it.

Standing How it shows What you do
Automated A red dot at the control's top left. Move it to override the lane.
Overridden A hollow red dot. The transport's Re-enable lights. Press Re-enable to give the value back to its lane.
Modulated A thin outer arc on the dial: the range that the modulator moves the value through. The status bar names the modulator. Set the centre of the range with the dial.
Held The arc or the fill in orange, and the holder's name under the control. Press the name to give the value back.
Waiting The value in italics, the arc dashed. Wait for the owner, or move it again.
Disabled The control at 40 % strength. The status bar says why. Do what the reason says.
No reading A hollow light. Nothing. The owner has not said.

#16.12 The display

  • Draw every mark from the device's values or readings, by the device's own law. Do not draw a likely picture.
  • When a reading is missing, say which one in the well. Do not draw it as zero.
  • Give a compact display at least one handle: the one parameter that you most often move. Give a whole display a handle for each parameter that the picture can show.
  • Number the handles 1, 2 and on, in order of importance.
  • Put axis labels inside the well, small and faint. Put one short reading at the top right.
  • Expanded and in a window, let the display take the room and keep the controls at their size, as an expanded EQ Eight does.
  • Move the picture only when its readings move. Respect a request for reduced motion.
  • A rack's picture is its chain list.

#16.13 What a device brings

A device brings these to the shell:
